- **D (an unhealthy container kept in service):** Control: `/health/live`, `/health/ready` and `/health/startup` (database round trip within 2 seconds, 503 when down), and a `HEALTHCHECK` on the liveness probe. The probes skip the CMS lookup in the proxy and return only status, latency and time (no host, version or error text).
- **Residual:** images are unsigned until cosign is approved (#67); known vulnerabilities without a fix are listed by `trivy image` and must be waived with an expiry in `infra/docker/trivyignore.yaml`.

### 3.38 The local Compose stack (built, PL6 session 1b; PL6-D-03)

`infra/compose/docker-compose.yml` and `pnpm dev:up` run the platform's services on a developer machine and in the `compose` CI job. Local only; never a deployment.

- **I (local secrets leaking):** Control: secrets are generated per machine into `infra/compose/.env.local` (mode 600, ignored by git and the image build context); none is a default or committed value; seeded passwords go to the never-committed `SEED_CREDENTIALS.md`. Local data is synthetic (the `dev` seed; IPB-11).
- **S and E (other hosts reaching the services):** Control: every published port is bound to `127.0.0.1`; Redis, MinIO, Keycloak, Unleash and PostgreSQL each require a generated password or token; containers drop all capabilities where the image allows it, and the apps run read-only as UID 10001.
- **T (a tampered image):** Control: every image is pinned by digest and was published at least 7 days before pinning (PL2-D-32); MinIO is built from source at the pinned commit, never from a mirror.
- **Residual:** Keycloak and the Temporal dev server run in development mode (no TLS) on loopback; the stack must never be exposed beyond the machine. Seeds stay refused in prod, dr and pre-prod by the seed guard regardless of this stack.

### 3.39 The shared Helm chart and admission policies (built, PL6 session 2; PL6-D-04)

`infra/helm/infrabyx-app` deploys every product workload; `infra/policy/kyverno` is the cluster's second line. Nothing is deployed from this repository.

- **E (a workload escaping its container):** Control: the chart's security context is not configurable (UID 10001, read-only root, no privilege escalation, every capability dropped, `RuntimeDefault` seccomp, no service-account token); Kyverno refuses pods without it, with host paths or host namespaces, and with tag-only images (`kyverno test`).
- **I (secrets in manifests):** Control: secrets reach pods only through the Secrets Store CSI mount (read-only), required from staging upwards; secret-named plain variables fail rendering there (`IPB-ENV-003`); the migration hook reads its URL from the mount (`--db-file`).
- **T (an unreviewed or unsigned image):** Control: images by digest only (schema and Kyverno); `verify-images` checks the keyless cosign signature and CycloneDX attestation and refuses a pod without them in every namespace except those labelled `infrabyx.dev/env` = local, dev, test or preview, where it audits (owner answer #67(1), PL6-D-16). The environment comes from the namespace label, which a pod cannot set, and an unlabelled namespace is enforced.
- **S and I (lateral movement):** Control: a default-deny NetworkPolicy in and out, peers listed per deployment, DNS only by default.
- **Tampering with the environment rules:** Control: a seed profile in prod, dr or pre-prod and a Supabase host from staging up fail rendering (`IPB-ENV-001`, `IPB-ENV-002`; `@IPB-01-AT-12`).
- **Residual:** a cluster administrator who mislabels a staging or production namespace as a lower environment turns enforcement into audit; namespace labels belong to the cluster bootstrap and its review. The chart cannot see the cluster; a product could deploy without Kyverno installed, which the IPB-01 Terraform `kubernetes` module (session 3) is to install. `helm diff` is not run.

### 3.40 OpenTofu platform modules (built, PL6 session 3a, AWS; PL6-D-05)

`infra/terraform/modules/aws` provisions a product environment when a product applies it; nothing is applied from this repository.

- **I (data at rest or in state):** Control: one rotated multi-region CMK encrypts buckets, database, cache, logs, secrets, node volumes and images; secrets are created without values (the bootstrap writes them), so state holds no secret on AWS; the example stack documents a locked, encrypted remote backend (state is SECRET class) and encrypts state and plans on the client with AES-GCM, `enforced = true`, the key derived from a run-time passphrase or a KMS key provider (owner answers #71, #72; PL6-D-16), so a leaked state file or backend bucket does not reveal provider-generated secrets such as the Memorystore AUTH string (§3.41).
- **T and repudiation (evidence altered or deleted):** Control: the AUDIT, REGULATED and CONFIDENTIAL buckets use Object Lock in compliance mode and replicate with their lock to the DR region; delete markers are not replicated; telemetry is locked for 180 days.
- **S and E (reaching or escalating in the environment):** Control: private subnets, a closed default security group, PostgreSQL and Redis reachable only from the VPC, TLS forced on both, IAM authentication, a private Kubernetes API by default, IMDSv2-only nodes, one IRSA role per service account scoped to its own subject, and break-glass off (MFA, SSM only) unless enabled.
- **Residency:** Control: any region other than ap-south-1 and ap-south-2 fails the plan without an ADR (IPB-01-36).
- **Residual:** provider lock files are not committed yet (exact version pins only); compliance-mode retention cannot be shortened once applied, so the defaults must be confirmed before a product applies them (#71).

### 3.41 OpenTofu platform modules on Azure and GCP (built, PL6 session 3b; PL6-D-06)

`infra/terraform/modules/azure` and `infra/terraform/modules/gcp` provision a product environment with the same contract as AWS when a product applies them; nothing is applied from this repository.

- **I (data at rest or in state):** Control: the platform key (HSM-backed, rotated) encrypts storage, PostgreSQL, the registry and, on GCP, Redis, the GKE secrets and the node disks; Azure nodes use encryption at host. No secret value is in state: Azure creates no secret, GCP creates secrets without versions. Residual: the Memorystore AUTH string is in state (state is SECRET class), and AKS etcd uses Azure's platform-managed key.
- **T and repudiation (evidence altered or deleted):** Control: the AUDIT, REGULATED and CONFIDENTIAL classes sit in their own geo-redundant accounts (Azure) or dual-region buckets (GCP) with locked retention; telemetry is retained 180 days; on Azure, `CanNotDelete` locks keep the accounts, vaults and the PostgreSQL server from deletion by any client (PL6-D-07). Residual: Azure policies are created unlocked and stay so until the product's next apply sets `immutability_locked`; `lock_mode` reports `unlocked` meanwhile.
- **S and E (reaching or escalating in the environment):** Control: PostgreSQL and Redis on private addresses only with TLS required; Entra ID or IAM authentication (AUTH on Memorystore); vault and storage firewalls deny by default; private Kubernetes control planes by default, local accounts off on AKS, Workload Identity with one identity per service account scoped to its own subject and to this environment's secrets only; break-glass off, and time-bound on GCP when on.
- **Residency:** Control: regions other than centralindia/southindia (Azure) and asia-south1/asia-south2 (GCP) fail the plan without an ADR (IPB-01-36).
- **Residual:** as on AWS, lock files are not committed yet and the retention defaults must be confirmed before a product applies them (#71, #72).

### 3.42 `apps/reference` api and worker (built, PL6 session 4a; PL6-D-08)

The reference API (NestJS 11) and its worker on the platform packages; the template products copy. Flows: browser → api (session cookie or bearer) → IDENTITY (code exchange, token verification), AUTHZ (one check per route), the kernel (forced RLS, the ledger and the outbox in one transaction); worker → QUEUE consumers and the same health probes.

- **S (a forged or replayed session):** Control: the authorisation code flow uses PKCE S256 with a state kept in a short-lived HttpOnly cookie and checked at the callback; tokens are verified by the IDENTITY port on every request (Keycloak JWKS, or the fixture's per-process HMAC key, which exists only in local, dev, test and preview); MFA is asserted for the roles that require it (IPB-02-02). Residual: the session cookie lives as long as the access token with no refresh; the fixture IdP has no brute-force protection (Keycloak's own applies with the real adapter).
- **T (cross-site requests on the cookie session):** Control: `SameSite=Lax` and `HttpOnly`, `Secure` on https; every state-changing route is a JSON `POST` or `DELETE` under the authentication guard; CORS is not enabled. Residual: a product that enables CORS for a separate web origin must add a CSRF token or `SameSite=Strict`.
- **R:** Control: every write runs in `kernel.mutate` with its ledger entry and its outbox event in the same transaction; retraction is an R2 request decided by a checker (never the maker, never an agent); deletion ends in the locked verb, refused to agents in the kernel and denied in the Cerbos policy.
- **I (what an error or a probe reveals):** Control: problems carry a code, a title and a safe detail, never SQL, stack traces or hosts; `/health/ready` names ports and adapters only; security headers (`nosniff`, `DENY`, `no-referrer`, `no-store`, a `default-src 'none'` CSP) on every response; `x-powered-by` off.
- **D:** Control: Express's default body limit; list reads capped at 200 rows; health probes time out at 2 s and answer 503 fail-closed. Residual: no rate limit at the API (the ingress or the IdP applies one).
- **E:** Control: the tenant context comes only from verified claims (`TenantContextInterceptor`); AUTHZ decides per route with the principal's roles and the tenant attribute; a sandbox-tenant owner reading a demo greeting gets 404 under RLS (NN-01).
- **Residual:** the fixture personas have fixed ids and roles by design (seed tenants only, IPB-11-26); products must never enable `APP_FIXTURE_ADAPTERS` outside the four fixture environments (the env check refuses it).

### 3.43 The reference gate endpoints (built, PL6 session 4b; PL6-D-09)

New flows in `apps/reference/api`: published content read from the CMS tables; the hello Vidhi skill (greeting text to the LLM port, an R2 proposal to the approvals engine); the tenant export (snapshot → archive in STORAGE, signed by KMS, downloaded by a signed link); the trust mirror fed by the TRUST port and served at `/trust`.

- **S and T (an agent acting beyond a maker):** Control: the task runs under a delegated agent context (`AGT_VIDHI`); at the tenant's default L1 only read tools run and the runtime refuses writes; the post is an R2 request the agent makes and a checker decides, executed under the checker's identity; the kernel's locked-verb guard and the Cerbos deny for agents stay in force. Residual: none beyond IPB-03's.
- **I (what reaches the model):** Control: greetings are INTERNAL and sent as data under a system prompt that says so; the fixture never leaves the process. Residual: the reference calls the LLM port directly, so the gateway's scrubber, BYOK and cost caps do not apply until a product wires the gateway (PL6-D-09; #74).
- **I (the archive and its link):** Control: the archive is built by the export service in the tenant's context under forced RLS, written to STORAGE by class, signed with the KMS key (C-22), downloaded through a short-lived signed link that is ledgered; only an owner exports directly and an admin's request waits for the owner. Residual: the fixture signer's key and the per-process pseudonym key are not production material.
- **T (a forged trust document):** Control: the mirror records only events it has not seen, verifies digests against the feed, and the Trust Centre shows PUBLISHED, PUBLIC documents only; gated documents are counted, never linked. Residual: the fixture feed is static.
- **D:** Control: content and trust reads are cheap kernel reads under `no-store`; the export limits per principal (5 open, 20 a day) hold; the worker's sweep is capped at 100 jobs a pass.
- **Residual:** LLM, KMS and TRUST answer `IPB-PORT-001` above staging until a product wires them, and `/health/ready` reports the service `degraded` meanwhile.

### 3.44 The local stack on the real adapters, and the outbox relay (built, PL6 session 4c; PL6-D-10)

`pnpm dev:up` makes the stack's Keycloak, MinIO and Unleash ready for `apps/reference` and runs the api and worker on them; the worker's outbox relay hands committed events to the QUEUE.

- **S (the local realm and its secrets):** Control: the realm is applied by the connectors' own `applyRealm` (ES256, PKCE, back-channel logout, admin-only user attributes); the client secrets are generated by Keycloak and written only to `.env.local` (never committed, mode 0600); the seed personas' passwords go to `SEED_CREDENTIALS.md` (never committed) and are generated per run. Residual: local only; the in-memory seed vault keeps no hash list between runs.
- **T and I (plain HTTP in the stack):** Control: the adapters' egress refuses non-HTTPS except to loopback with the explicit flag, which the api sets only in the four fixture environments; the containers therefore use the host network and loopback ports, and no Compose service name is ever allowed. Residual: traffic between the containers and the services is plain on the loopback interface of the developer's machine or the CI runner.
- **E (the relay reading across tenants):** Control: the relay lists tenants as a service principal holding the read-only platform role and nothing else, and reads or marks each tenant's outbox only in that tenant's own context under forced RLS; every batch writes one ledger entry in the tenant; the job carries ids and the envelope only. Residual: the platform role lets the relay read every tenant's row in the platform-scope tables that admit `PLT_*` readers, more than the tenant list it needs; a reader role for product outboxes (#74) would narrow it.
- **D:** Control: batches of 100 rows per tenant and schema per pass; `skip locked` keeps two workers from blocking each other; the queue de-duplicates on the event id.

### 3.45 The reference worker on Temporal (built, PL6 session 4d; PL6-D-11)

The reference api and worker use the stack's Temporal for the durable timers of `@infrabyx/workflow` (approval reminders, escalation, expiry; Work Inbox escalation): the api starts one `ipbTimer` workflow per timer, the worker runs the `ipb-timers` task queue and fires each timer by id in its own tenant; the PostgreSQL poller reconciles.

- **I and D (payloads and ids at the engine):** Control: every payload passes the C-16 codec (`FieldEnvelope` with the KMS key `WORKFLOW_CODEC_KEY_ID`, AAD bound to the namespace); workflow ids are timer ids (`ipb-timer.<tenant>.<kind>.<ref>`, identifiers only, PL2-C-04) and the input is the id and an instant; the adapter refuses ids that read as personal data and task queues outside the prefix.
- **E (a fired timer acting in the wrong tenant):** Control: the activity parses the tenant from the timer id and fires through `WorkflowTimers.fire` in that tenant's service context under forced RLS; a timer that is not due, cancelled or already fired is a no-op; the handler re-checks the record it acts on.
- **T (plain gRPC in the local stack):** Control: the connection is plain only to the loopback address of the Compose stack; a deployment sets mTLS or an API key through `TemporalAuth` (Temporal Cloud takes INTERNAL inputs only, PL2-D-16).
- **D (Temporal down):** Control: the readiness probe reports WORKFLOW `degraded`, never `down`; timers still fire through the poller within a minute; the worker's Temporal loop logs and ends without taking the queue consumers with it.
- **Residual:** the local dev server keeps its state in memory, so timers started before a restart fire through the poller only; the deployed engine (session 6 onwards) is durable.
- **Supply chain (R1-D-60):** the SDK is `@temporalio/*` 1.25.0, whose `core-bridge` ships Rust `zip` 9.0.5. The four `zip` 8.5.1 findings of 1.24.0 (CVE-2026-115905, -115907, -115908, -115909) and their waivers are gone. The API image's body-parser 2.4.0 and multer 2.5.0, set by overrides, close CVE-2026-111181 and CVE-2026-113872 and their waivers.

### 3.46 The reference web app, a backend-for-frontend (built, PL6 session 5; PL6-D-12)

`apps/reference/web` is the browser's only origin: it proxies sign-in to the reference api and renders every page from server-side api calls that carry the person's session cookie.

- **S (session theft):** Control: the session is one HttpOnly, SameSite=Lax cookie set by the api through the web's proxy on the web origin (Secure on https); the browser never holds a token in script and never calls the api; the api verifies the token, its roles and the second factor on every call (IPB-02-AT-02).
- **T (cross-site requests):** Control: every POST goes to a route handler of the web that refuses another origin (Origin, or Sec-Fetch-Site) and answers 303 to a path of this origin only; the nonce CSP with `form-action 'self'` and `frame-ancestors 'none'`.
- **I (what the web passes on):** Control: the server-side calls forward the session cookie and nothing else; problems are shown by title and code, never a stack or a host; the export download is a short-lived signed link minted per click and ledgered (C-22), with the archive digest in a response header so the client can check the file.
- **E (the web as a confused deputy):** Control: the web holds no credential of its own and calls the api only as the person; the api's AUTHZ and maker-checker decide (the assistant drafts, a checker approves).
- **T and I (web to api in transit):** Control: `APP_API_URL` must be https except to loopback in local, dev, test and preview (IPB-SEC-018).
- **Residual:** the `pages/` route serves the product's default pack whether or not the pack is published (its pages still pass the CMS maker-checker); a product publishes its pack and resolves it by host.

### 3.47 The product scaffold and its CI registry (built, PL6 session 6b; PL6-D-14)

`scripts/scaffold-product.mjs` writes a new product repository from this one; the `scaffold` CI job installs it from a local npm registry that serves the packed platform tarballs.

- **T (a product built on unpinned or substituted code):** Control: every `@infrabyx/*` dependency is an exact version, the release-age rule and Renovate come with the template, and the OpenTofu modules are referenced at a fixed platform commit, never a branch (IPB-01-38).
- **I (a credential in the product repository):** Control: the product's `.npmrc` names the registry only; CI supplies the read token through `actions/setup-node`; `.gitignore` excludes `.env*` and `SEED_CREDENTIALS.md`.
- **S and T (the CI registry):** Control: it listens on loopback only inside one job, serves only the `@infrabyx` scope from tarballs packed in that job, with SHA-256 integrity in each packument; nothing it serves leaves the job.
- **Residual:** the scaffold copies the reference apps' fixture adapters and seed personas, which run only in local, dev, test and preview (PL1-D-06); a product wires its real adapters before staging. The template does not yet carry the git hooks (gitleaks, the env and credential guard, commitlint) or the secret-scanning job; the product copies them from this repository on day one.

### 3.48 The outbox relay as a service reader (built, R1 item 1; R1-D-01)

`OutboxRelay` in `@infrabyx/tenancy` relays module outboxes to the QUEUE as `SVC_OUTBOX_RELAY`, the one service reader that also writes.

- **E (the relay reading or changing more than its outboxes):** Control: its database role starts with no grant; `tenancy.register_outbox()` grants select of the relayed columns and update of `dispatched_at` and `attempts` on one registered `<schema>.outbox`, and `audit/0002` the ledger append; any other table or column is refused by PostgreSQL (`42501`, proven in the integration tests). The kernel admits only `<schema>.outbox.dispatch` for this reader, in a tenant context.
- **T (marking another tenant's events dispatched):** Control: there is no platform-scope update policy; every update runs in the row's own tenant context under the outbox's tenant policy, so a batch can only touch its tenant's rows.
- **R (an unrecorded relay):** Control: the cross-tenant discovery read is ledgered per tenant (`platform.service_read`), and each batch writes `<schema>.outbox.dispatch` in its tenant's ledger in the same transaction.
- **S (registering a table that is not an outbox):** Control: `register_outbox` is executable by `app_migrate` only, validates the schema name, the columns and forced RLS, and grants on that schema's `outbox` table only.
- **Residual:** the event envelope is read by the relay (it must be, to deliver it); envelopes carry no secrets by the IPB-10 event contract.
- **E (the read-only role writing through the relay role; found by Udofin, PR-UDF-016):** `tenancy/0003` granted `svc_outbox_relay` to `app_ro` and `app_rw` with the default INHERIT, so both held its writes (the ledger append, `dispatched_at` and `attempts`) without switching. Control: `tenancy/0004` revokes it from `app_ro` and makes every `svc_*` membership of `app_rw` and `app_ro` INHERIT FALSE, SET TRUE; `app_ro` and `app_rw` are NOINHERIT, so later grants default the same (R1-D-11). The integration test proves a login holding only `app_ro` is refused every write and the switch to `svc_outbox_relay` (42501), and that no role reachable from a read-only or auditor role holds a write privilege.
- **D (events that never leave their table; PR-LDC-35, R1-D-112):** registration was opt-in per module, and none of the 15 platform outboxes was registered, so their events were never relayed. Control: each package's own migration registers its outbox (the same column grants and discovery policy as above, nothing wider); `ipb-check` fails a package that creates an outbox without registering it; the reference API suite checks the registered list equals every outbox table. Rows written before the fix are relayed on the first pass after migration; consumers are idempotent on the event id.

### 3.49 The visual block editor (built, R1 item 2; PR-038, R1-D-02)

The Studio page editor edits a page's blocks through forms (`@infrabyx/ui/studio` `BlockEditor` and `blockHandlers`, with the block forms and the rich text codec from `@infrabyx/cms` `BLOCK_EDITOR`). It is a new way into the same save: every operation becomes one `CmsStudio.saveDraft` call.

- **T (markup or script through rich text):** Control: the editor's text is turned into portable text, never HTML, and `sanitisePortableText` runs on the result, then again at save and at render (IPB-04-15); link targets go through `safeHref`, so a `javascript:` or other non-allow-listed link is dropped (unit-tested). Escapes only change how text reads, not what it may contain.
- **T (a block outside the library or a hand-made body):** Control: `saveDraft` parses the whole body against the block schemas as before (`IPB-CMS-001`), so a posted field the form did not offer, a block of an unknown type or a second hero is refused; the JSON fallback for blocks without a form has the same check.
- **T (two editors overwriting each other's blocks by position):** Control: every block form carries the revision it was rendered from; the handler refuses a post against an older revision.
- **E (editing without the right role, or on a deleted item):** Control: the handler only calls the CMS service, which checks the editor roles and platform scope on every save; the console's AUTHZ gate covers the new action like the section's other actions; a deleted item shows no editor.
- **R:** every operation is a new immutable revision with a summary (`Block moved (block 2)`) and the save's ledger entry; approvals are voided as for any edit.
- **Residual:** none new; the editor reuses the save path, its checks and its audit.

### 3.50 The Ecosystem inbox (built, R1 G2; R1-D-15)

`EcosystemInbox` in `@infrabyx/tenancy` stores inbound Ecosystem events in the consuming module's `eco_inbox` before processing (`tenancy.create_ecosystem_inbox`, migration `tenancy/0006`) and processes them in `seq` order per subject.

- **T and R (a replayed or doubled event acting twice):** Control: the event is stored first, unique by `(tenant, source, id)` and by `(tenant, type, business key)`; a duplicate of either is acknowledged and never handed to the handler; an event at or below the subject's position is skipped; every receive, step, failure, break and repositioning is ledgered in the same transaction.
- **T (out-of-order events applied in the wrong order):** Control: an event ahead of the next `seq` is parked, never processed early; after 15 minutes the product's replay fetches the gap, and a gap still open becomes a break the product raises as `ReconciliationBreakRaised`; only an operator's `setPosition` from the owner's snapshot reopens the subject.
- **S (a sandbox event in production):** Control: an event whose `environment` differs from the inbox's is refused (`IPB-API-005`), as is an envelope that does not validate (consent or legal basis with personal data included).
- **I and E (one tenant's events seen or changed by another):** Control: both tables have forced row-level security by the consuming tenant; `app_rw` may select, insert and update, never delete; the function that creates them is executable by `app_migrate` only and validates the schema name.
- **D (a failing handler blocking a subject):** Control: the handler's transaction rolls back; the attempt is counted in its own transaction; after `maxAttempts` (5) the event is `failed` and the subject waits for an operator instead of retrying without end.
- **Residual:** signature verification of the inbound delivery (Standard Webhooks, RFC 9421) happens before `receive`, in the product's gateway; the detached JWS of regulated messages is G5. Inbox rows are kept until the product decides their retention (no purge is built; retention is the owner's).

### 3.51 JWS for regulated messages (built, R1 G5; R1-D-08, R1-D-17)

`signRegulatedMessage` and `verifyRegulatedMessage` in `@infrabyx/crypto` sign and check the detached JWS that Ecosystem Spec §3.6 stores with each regulated message (`isRegulatedMessage` in `@infrabyx/contracts/events` names them).

- **S and T (a forged or altered regulated message):** Control: ES256 only, through an allow-list that refuses `none`, every `HS*` and every other algorithm; the signature covers the JCS form of the whole CloudEvent; the key is in KMS and named by `kid`; a DER signature, an attached payload or non-canonical base64url is refused.
- **S (algorithm or key confusion through the header):** Control: the protected header must hold exactly `alg`, `kid`, `typ`, `iat` and `jti`; `b64`, `crit`, `jku`, `jwk`, `x5u`, `x5c` and any other member are refused; keys come only from the verifier's own key ring, never from the message, and the ring takes EC P-256 keys only.
- **R and T (a replayed message):** Control: `iat` must lie within five minutes back and one minute ahead, and a `jti` is accepted once (the replay store); a message that fails any other check never claims its `jti`, so a forgery cannot block the real one.
- **D (a key rotation dropping messages):** Control: the key ring holds the current and the previous key, each with its validity window; messages signed during the overlap verify with either.
- **Residual:** `MemoryReplayStore` is per process; a product with several verifier instances backs `ReplayStore` with shared storage, or relies on the Ecosystem inbox's `(source, id)` uniqueness as the durable second line. RFC 9421 transport signatures are the product gateway's.

### 3.52 Scheduled per-tenant jobs (built, PR-UDF-013; R1-D-12)

`TenantSweep` in `@infrabyx/tenancy` lists the tenants for a product's scheduled jobs, and `AuditSweep` in `@infrabyx/audit` runs the IPB-02-51 ledger jobs (weekly verification, daily anchor) for each.

- **I and E (a job seeing more than its tenant):** Control: the list is a `SVC_TENANT_SWEEP` read whose database role selects only `id` and `status` of `tenancy.tenant` (anything else is 42501, tested); each job then runs in its tenant's own context under RLS, as the job's service principal, and sees only that tenant's rows (tested: one tenant visible per run).
- **R (an unrecorded sweep):** Control: the list read is ledgered in every tenant it lists (`platform.service_read`); the job's own writes are ledgered under its principal; a break found by verification is ledgered and raises `LedgerChainBroken`, as for one tenant.
- **E (a job holding reader rights or reader limits):** Control: the reader and the job principal are separate; a context holding a service-reader role is read-only in the kernel, so the job runs as `svc_tenant_job` (or the product's own principal), never as the reader; the reader's role is switch-only (R1-D-11).
- **D (one tenant blocking the rest):** Control: a tenant's failure is reported by its error code and the sweep continues.
- **Residual:** scheduling, retries and alerting on `broken` or `failed` are the product worker's (`docs/product-guide.md` §7c).

### 3.53 Customer sign-in: one-time codes and DPoP (built, #84; R1-D-18, R1-D-19)

`CustomerIdentityPort` signs in end users who are not staff, in a customer realm (Keycloak, with the `ipb-customer-otp` extension) or organisation (Zitadel) per product. Codes go out through NOTIFY; sessions are bound to a per-device ES256 key by DPoP and checked by `verifyDpopProof`.

- **S (guessing or brute-forcing a code):** Control: six digits from a CSPRNG, 300 s, 5 tries per code; at most 10 codes per 10 minutes per account, 30 s apart; back-off after 5 consecutive wrong codes and a 15-minute lockout after 10, decided before the IdP is called; Keycloak's brute-force protection behind it. The extension compares in constant time and consumes the challenge on success.
- **S (a stolen access or refresh token):** Control: requests need `Authorization: DPoP` and a proof with `ath` by an active device key of the token's user (and on Keycloak the key in `cnf.jkt`); a refresh needs a proof by the device's key and the current refresh token; a current refresh token with another key revokes the device; a used refresh token is dead (Keycloak rotation without reuse, Zitadel rotation, and the platform's compare-and-set).
- **S and T (forged or replayed proofs):** Control: ES256 only; strict header, key and claim shapes; `htm`, `htu` and `ath` bound; `iat` within 60 s back and 30 s ahead; each `jti` claimed once per key in a replay store shared by replicas (`RedisReplayStore`), only after the proof verifies.
- **D and S (the replay store's own Redis, PR-UDF-032, R1-D-47):** `RedisReplayStore.fromUrl` opens the platform's ioredis connection to the product's Redis or Valkey (`rediss://` with TLS above development; AUTH token from SECRETS, never in the URL there). Control: commands are not queued while disconnected and time out (2 s by default), so an outage makes `claim` throw and the proof is refused, never accepted; keys are the SHA-256 of the `jti` under a prefix, with the proof's TTL. Residual: a Redis outage stops customer sign-in and refresh until it is back; a flushed or failed-over Redis without persistence forgets claims, so a proof captured in the last 60 s could be replayed once (bounded by the `iat` window and `ath`).
- **S and E (taking over an account by changing its login, PR-UDF-034, R1-D-51):** Control: a change needs a signed-in device key, a code sent to the current login (which must be this account's) and one sent to the new login (which must be nobody's); both are six digits from the CSPRNG, kept only as salted SHA-256 digests with the new login's digest, never the codes or the new login; the confirmation must come from the same key, within the code lifetime, under the sign-in guard's attempt limits; a login-change challenge can never be used as a sign-in code. The change and a retirement are ledgered by the account's hash only. Retiring signs every device out and forgets it, disables the account at the IdP and frees the login, so a recycled number reaches a new, empty account. Residual: a DB reader could brute-force a stored digest (10^6 codes) within the code's 5-minute life; the database is CONFIDENTIAL and readable only by the API's roles.
- **S (a recycled login resolved to the account it was retired from, R1-D-166, R1-D-167):** Zitadel's user search is eventually consistent, so just after a retirement or a login change it can still list the old account under the login. Control: the Zitadel customer adapter uses a search hit only after `GET /v2/users/{id}`, which brings Zitadel's user projection up to date before reading, shows that account active with the login's username now; a stale hit is skipped, and an account not yet in the search is found by its own id. A code is therefore never issued for a retired or moved account, and a lookup Zitadel cannot confirm fails rather than guesses. Keycloak reads its database directly and is not affected. Residual: Zitadel only logs a failed projection trigger, so the read-your-writes property is Zitadel's behaviour, not a documented contract [VERIFY]; a contract case pins the search to its pre-retirement answer on every run.
- **D (a new account's first code refused while Zitadel's projection catches up, R1-D-171, R1-D-172):** Zitadel's session API checks the user against its user projection as it is, so a session for an account created or changed a moment ago can be refused as not found. Control: the adapter reads a new account by id (`GET /v2/users/{id}`, which brings the projection up to date) before its first session, and on a 404 from `POST /v2/sessions` reads the account by id once and tries once more; a second refusal stays `IPB-PORT-002`, and no account is created or chosen on a guess. **Residual:** if Zitadel's own trigger fails, the sign-in fails as unavailable and the customer tries again.
- **S (a code phished to another device):** Control: the code is bound to the key that asked for it; another key's proof at `verifyChallenge` is refused.
- **E (a customer factor satisfying a staff check):** Control: customer realms and organisations are separate from staff realms, with their own issuer and audience; customer tokens carry product roles only (staff roles are refused) and never count as MFA (IPB-02-02); the realm has no `amr` mapper.
- **I (codes or logins leaking):** Control: the IdP keeps only a SHA-256 of `<challenge>:<code>` (Keycloak) or its own hashed code (Zitadel); the code reaches the product API over TLS from the authenticated client call and leaves only through NOTIFY's `sendOneTimeCode`, which stores `[redacted]` and never the address; the platform's tables hold no code, no login (accounts by IdP id, hashed in the guard) and refresh tokens only as SHA-256; ledger entries carry no codes. The extension refuses public clients and clients not marked `ipb.customer.otp`, so only the product API, with its secret, can ask for a code.
- **I and E (one tenant's customers seen by another):** Control: accounts are per tenant (usernames derived from the tenant and the login); `connectors/0004` has forced RLS by tenant, select, insert and update for `app_rw`, and delete only under the retention floors of `connectors/0005`.
- **R:** every guard decision, challenge, attempt, device creation, refresh and revocation is ledgered.
- **I (sign-in state kept longer than needed; owner answer #86, R1-D-23):** Control: a per-tenant job deletes challenges 24 h after expiry, guard rows once their lockout window has ended, and devices 12 months after last use, each period a setting. Unbinding a device or closing the account deletes devices at once. Restrictive delete policies (`connectors/0005`) keep floors below the settings, so no caller can delete a live challenge, an active lock or a device in use. Every deletion is ledgered.
- **D (code flooding or lockout of a victim):** Control: the per-account limits cap SMS cost and annoyance; a lockout is timed (15 minutes), never permanent; the product's edge limits per IP (IPB-02 §02.4.6.2) sit in front.
- **Residual:** Zitadel does not bind tokens to the key, so a Zitadel access token is bearer at the IdP until it expires (300 s) and its refresh token survives the platform's sign-out at the IdP; both are refused by the platform. Keycloak's 1-second quick-login check can lock a customer for 60 s after two very fast tries. The image is built on the upstream Keycloak 26.8.1-1 rebuild (pinned 30 Oct 2026, once 7 days old), which clears CVE-2026-114406; five base findings remain waived until 20 Nov 2026 (`infra/docker/trivyignore.yaml`: two in UBI 9's OpenJDK and two in its `sqlite-libs`, none with a fixed build yet, one in Keycloak's Guava jar), extended by the owner (OWNER-QUESTIONS #93, R1-D-36) with a re-scan on 6 Nov and a fixed image pinned once 7 days old. Exposure is low: outbound TLS only to configured brokers, no untrusted fonts rendered, no Guava data deserialised from requests. Retention of the sign-in tables is the owner's (OWNER-QUESTIONS #86).
- **Accepted risk (owner, 7 Nov 2026; OWNER-QUESTIONS #124, R1-D-109): CVE-2026-91403** (OpenJDK eMudhra distrust in the UBI 9 base of the Keycloak image; Red Hat `will_not_fix`, so no Keycloak image will clear it). Compensating controls: the IdP makes outbound TLS calls only to configured identity brokers; it renders no untrusted fonts; it deserialises no Guava data from requests. Waived to 20 Dec 2026 with the three other base findings (CVE-2026-119373, CVE-2026-119586 in `sqlite-libs`; CVE-2026-102554 in Guava 33.6.0), and reviewed at every re-scan (next 15 Dec); the waiver for CVE-2026-102554 goes as soon as a Keycloak image carries Guava 33.7.2.

### 3.54 The `KYC` and `VIDEO_MEET` ports (interfaces and fakes, PR-FBX-01; R1-D-21)

The interfaces and their contract cases, with in-memory fakes. No adapter yet (OWNER-QUESTIONS #87, #88), so no data leaves a product through these ports today.

- **I (identity data leaking through the port):** Control: every KYC request schema is REGULATED; adapters must be `IN`; files move as STORAGE references with SHA-256, never inline; results never carry an Aadhaar number (last four digits only).
- **I (a V-CIP recording kept by the vendor or in a shared bucket):** Control: a CONFIDENTIAL or REGULATED room must declare `providerRetention: "none"` and a recording target (the schema refuses otherwise); `fetchRecording` returns a hash manifest, never media; per-tenant target routing is deferred (#83), so V-CIP stays off.
- **S (a V-CIP call from outside India or from an untrusted network):** Control: `joinToken` refuses (`IPB-SEC-002`) a country outside `participantCountries`, an anonymised origin, an official outside `officialNetworks`, and a participant in another role; the origin comes from the product's GEO check.
- **T and R (a forged vendor event or an altered recording):** Control: webhooks are triggers only; the session or room is read back from the vendor; the manifest lists every artefact by SHA-256 with its own hash, and the product ledgers it.
- **Residual:** the cases prove the contract on the fakes only; each adapter must pass them on its vendor sandbox before routing selects it.

### 3.55 The Digio KYC adapter (`digio-kyc`, built behind a flag; R1-D-24)

DigiLocker documents and Aadhaar Offline e-KYC through Digio's KYC API. Off (`int.kyc.digio.enabled`) and `unproven` (#147) until the owner's account exists; V-CIP through Digio waits for #90.

- **I (identity documents sent or kept where they should not be):** Control: the adapter is `IN` and REGULATED; files are read from STORAGE and checked against their SHA-256 before upload; downloaded files are written REGULATED under the call's tenant, so a pull without a tenant is refused; nothing goes to Digio while the flag is off.
- **I (the Aadhaar number leaving the vendor):** Control: results carry the last four digits only; a reply carrying a twelve-digit run is refused as a vendor error.
- **S and R (a consent session reused, or used for another customer):** Control: a session is claimed once in a replay store, and only after Digio reports it complete. The product binds the session id to its customer when it creates the KYC request.
- **S (credentials):** Control: HTTP Basic with the client id and secret from SECRETS; egress allows Digio's host only; 401 and 403 surface as credential errors, not retries.
- **Residual:** the vendor facts are checked on a stub only until the sandbox leg runs (#147).

### 3.56 Statutory audit capture (`audit/0003`; PR-FBX-10 item 2, R1-D-25)

PostgreSQL captures every insert, update and delete on registered books-of-account tables, with values and context, in a per-tenant hash chain the daily anchor signs.

- **T and R (a change to the books that leaves no trace):** Control: row triggers enabled ALWAYS on every registered table, TRUNCATE refused, and the capture written by a SECURITY DEFINER function whose owner role nobody holds. Event triggers refuse dropping or disabling any part. The chain, the head and `capture_problems()` are verified daily, and the anchor signs the head.
- **T (the captured history edited):** Control: update, delete and truncate on `audit.capture` are refused always, for every role. A superuser who first disables the guard leaves a chain break and a reported problem.
- **I (captured values are copies of REGULATED data):** Control: `audit.capture` has forced RLS by tenant (read only), its value columns are classed REGULATED, and columns encrypted by field crypto stay ciphertext in the copy. The rows are kept as the ledger is.
- **E (the capture writer misused):** Control: `capture_writer` is NOLOGIN, NOINHERIT and granted to no role; the read-only-role check covers it. The capture function takes no argument and writes only what the trigger sees.
- **D (a capture failure blocks the books):** Control: settings that do not fit a column are recorded as absent rather than refusing the write; a row without `tenant_id` is refused, which registration prevents.
- **Residual:** superuser access to PostgreSQL, as for the ledger; event triggers do not fire in replica mode, so DDL done there is caught by the next daily verification instead.

### 3.57 Branch context (`tenancy/0007`, `audit/0004`; PR-FBX-10 item 1, R1-D-26)

Products scope rows by branch within a tenant through `tenancy.branch_allowed()` and the kernel's branch settings.

- **E (a user reads or writes another branch's rows):** Control: the policy's branch condition denies by default; the branches come only from the verified context, the kernel sets them, and a callback cannot change them. A comma cannot appear in an id, so a list cannot be widened.
- **E (head-office scope granted by accident):** Control: `allBranches` is an explicit flag in the context, never inferred from an empty list (an empty list sees nothing).
- **R (which branch a change was made in):** Control: the ledger entry and the statutory capture record the branch; the ledger chain covers it.
- **T (an exported audit trail that cannot be re-verified):** Control: `REF-EXPORT-1.3` exports `branch_id`, and the offline verifier hashes it only when set, exactly as the ledger chain does.
- **Residual:** branch scoping is as strong as the product's policies; `rls-lint` checks the tenant condition, not the branch one. Tenant isolation is unchanged.

### 3.58 Inbound idempotency (`tenancy/0008`; PR-FBX-10 item 4, R1-D-27)

Product APIs store each POST and PATCH response under the client's key for 24 hours and replay it for an identical retry.

- **T (a replay used to get another request's response):** Control: a replay must match the stored route and the canonical request hash, else 409; keys are per tenant under forced RLS.
- **I (stored responses):** Control: response bodies are classed REGULATED, readable only in their tenant, and deleted after the TTL (only expired rows can be deleted).
- **D (duplicate work under concurrency):** Control: one request per key at a time (advisory lock, 409 IPB-API-011); the key commits with the change.
- **I and S (one principal served another's response; PR-UDF-033 (2), R1-D-48):** every borrower of a product shares its tenant. Control: a key belongs to the principal that first stored it (`tenancy/0015`), and another principal presenting it is refused with 409 (IPB-PORT-004). The interceptor also hashes the route's parameters, so one key cannot replay across two resources of one route (PR-FBX-21 (2)).
- **T and E (joined mutations; PR-UDF-033 (1), PR-FBX-21 (1), R1-D-48):** `kernel.ambient` lets a service's `mutate` run in the handler's transaction. Control: a joined call must have the ambient call's tenant, principal, scope and break-glass grant, and run as the app role. Each runs in a kernel savepoint with its own `app.*` settings and its own ledger check; a failure rolls back only its writes. Joined calls run one at a time. Reads join only with `joinReads` (§3.133), and a joined read stays read-only. Residual: rows written before `tenancy/0015` have no principal and keep the per-tenant check until they expire (24 hours).
- **Residual:** a client that reuses a key after expiry starts a new request, as IPB-10 intends.

### 3.59 Break-glass visible to the tenant (`tenancy/0009`; PR-FBX-10 item 3, R1-D-28)

Each tenant sees every break-glass grant used on its data, and is notified on first use.

- **R (staff access to a tenant's data unknown to the tenant):** Control: the kernel records each use of a grant in the tenant before the transaction runs, for reads as for writes; the tenant reads the record (who asked, who approved, why, until when, how often); a notice event is raised once per grant.
- **T (the record hidden or altered):** Control: the application role can only read it; writes go through two SECURITY DEFINER functions that act on the transaction's own tenant and an active grant only.
- **I (the grant's reason shown to the tenant):** Control: by design, the reason for touching the tenant's data is the tenant's to see; it is CONFIDENTIAL and readable only in that tenant.
- **Residual:** a use is recorded when the kernel runs; direct database access by a superuser is outside it, as for the ledger.

### 3.60 External read role (`tenancy/0010`; PR-FBX-10 item 5, R1-D-29)

A named external grantee (statutory auditor, inspector) reads one tenant's data under one grant, as the SELECT-only `app_external` role.

- **E (an external grantee reading or changing more than granted):** Control: the role holds SELECT only and belongs to no role (rls-lint `external-read`); the kernel runs its reads read-only, as that role, only for an active grant that is the principal's own and in the tenant; every table it can read has forced RLS and a RESTRICTIVE policy calling `tenancy.external_allows`, which limits rows to the grant's entities, branches and period whatever the table's other policies; columns are the product's explicit grants.
- **S (a grant used by someone else or in another tenant):** Control: the grant row must match the context's grant id, principal and tenant on every transaction (`IPB-TRUST-003`).
- **E (a grant created or widened by one person):** Control: a second ORG_OWNER or ORG_ADMIN approves it (`IPB-TRUST-005` on self-approval); 90 days at most; revocation takes effect on the next transaction.
- **R (an unrecorded read):** Control: the kernel accepts an external principal's transaction only from `ExternalReads`, which ledgers each read (grant, table, row count), empty ones too, before it returns the rows.
- **Residual:** the ledger names the table and row count, not the query text; a product's restrictive policy must cover the right columns (entity, branch, date) for each table it opens, which rls-lint checks for presence, not meaning.

### 3.61 Saved table views and the shell's search (`config/0002`, `ui`; PR-FBX-09, R1-D-32)

People save a table's filters, sort and columns for themselves or share them; the command palette searches what they may open.

- **I (a personal view or layout read by someone else):** Control: forced RLS on `app.actor_id` keeps personal views and table layouts to their owner, in their tenant; `SavedViews` returns a shared view only to its audience (the team ids the product vouches for, the named tenant roles, or the tenant), and a view the caller cannot see answers 404.
- **T (a shared view changed by its audience):** Control: only the owner edits or deletes a view, and an ORG_ADMIN or ORG_OWNER a roles or tenant one, in the service and in the update and delete policies; ownership never moves; updates carry the version read (409 on a stale one); every change, including a replaced default, is ledgered.
- **E (sharing wider than allowed):** Control: roles and tenant views, and the tenant default, need ORG_ADMIN or ORG_OWNER; a team view needs membership; platform, agent and service roles are never an audience; `ux.tables.saved_views_shared` off refuses and hides shared views; agents and platform-scope contexts are refused.
- **I (identifiers in search or stored filters):** Control: `searchRefusal` (`contracts`) refuses Aadhaar (any twelve digits, a Verhoeff VID), Luhn-valid card numbers and PANs in the browser, before any search provider runs, and in every saved filter value and view name; refusals and the palette's analytics event carry the pattern class only; the query goes by POST, never in a URL, and a refused one is not written back into the page.
- **I (search results beyond the caller's rights):** Control: each `SearchProvider` trims at query time; a failing provider's error is dropped (it may echo the query) and only its id is reported.
- **Residual:** shared views are visible to any tenant session at the database level (the audience trim is in `SavedViews`), and team membership is the product's claim; a view's name and filters are CONFIDENTIAL text a person chose, kept until they or an administrator delete it.

### 3.16 Cross-tenant service readers (owner answer #22, PL3-D-21)

Background workers that must find due work across tenants (the workflow timer poller, the NOTIFY dispatcher, the one-click unsubscribe link, the export sweep) read through `ServiceReader` in `@infrabyx/tenancy` as one of the `SVC_` roles in `SERVICE_READER_ROLES` (`contracts/roles`).

- **E:** a background identity reading or changing more than it needs. Control: each role maps to its own database role (`svc_scheduler`, `svc_notify_dispatch`, `svc_notify_unsubscribe`, `svc_export_sweep`, `svc_ai_run_sweep`, `svc_ai_index_sweep`, `svc_vidhi_sched`, `svc_tenant_sweep`, created by the owning package's migration), granted `SELECT` on the named columns of its tables only and nothing else (one table each, except `svc_export_sweep`: jobs, archives and the deletion marker, ids and times only, under platform-scope read policies limited `to` that role; PL3-D-25); the kernel runs a platform-scope read as that role (`SET LOCAL ROLE`), so a query on any other table or column fails with `42501`, and forced RLS still applies.
- **E:** a reader approving, writing or impersonating. Control: the kernel refuses every mutation by a reader except the ledger entry recording its own read, in a tenant context (`IPB-SEC-005`); its context must be a service principal with exactly one reader role, no `PLT_*` role, no `act` and no break-glass; a token claiming a reader role is refused (`contextFromClaims`), so readers exist only in-process.
- **R:** an unrecorded cross-tenant read. Control: the query must select `tenant_id`; each read that returned rows writes one `platform.service_read` ledger entry in every tenant it touched (actor = the reader principal, resource = the table) and calls the operational `onServiceRead` hook with the row count per tenant. An empty read returns no tenant data and writes nothing.
- **Residual:** the platform-scope RLS read policies on these tables still admit PLT\_\* principals (people, under IPB-02-11), as before.
- **E (inherited reader privileges, PR-UDF-016):** until `tenancy/0004` every `svc_*` role was inherited by `app_ro` and `app_rw`, so their column grants and platform-scope policies applied without the kernel switching roles. Control: the memberships are switch-only (INHERIT FALSE, SET TRUE) and the two roles NOINHERIT, so a reader's grants apply only inside the kernel's `SET LOCAL ROLE` (R1-D-11).

## 5. CI and supply chain

- **T:** a compromised action or dependency. Controls: X-12; Renovate as the single updater; `ipb:check` refuses regressions; dependency and image ages recorded in DECISIONS (PL2-D-32, PL2-D-37).
- **E:** over-privileged release token. Open risk R-01 below; the GitHub App token replaces `NPM_PUBLISH_TOKEN` before production (PL1-D-21, PL2-D-42).
- **T (a substituted release artefact):** Control: the release job signs the BOM with cosign keyless (the release workflow's GitHub OIDC identity, Fulcio, Rekor) and verifies it before publishing, then attests each published tarball as the registry serves it with the BOM as CycloneDX predicate, verifies it and attaches the bundles to the package's release (owner answer #67(1), IPB-02-35, `@IPB-01-AT-18`); cosign is installed from the Go module proxy at a pinned version, checked by Go's checksum database.
- **I (what signing discloses):** every signature writes the repository, workflow and ref to the public Rekor log, permanently. Accepted for this public repository; `docs/product-guide.md` §6 tells private product repositories to sign with a KMS key if their names and release cadence must not be public.
- **I:** secrets in commits. Controls: gitleaks in CI with a reviewed baseline, the pre-commit env and credential guard, generated test tokens (no literal tokens in tests).
- **T:** a published package that a product cannot load (the 0.1.0 `@temporalio/proto` import). Control: `consumer-smoke` installs the packed tarballs with plain npm and imports every export with plain Node, in CI and before every publish (PL3-D-02).
- **T:** a third-party package published minutes before a release running its install scripts in the release job, beside its signing identity (found 29 Oct 2026: release run 130 resolved `@types/estree` two minutes after it was published). Control: `consumer-smoke` resolves with npm `--before` set 7 days back, the same rule pnpm's `minimumReleaseAge` applies to the workspace (PL2-D-32, R1-D-30).
- **D:** CI minutes exhausted on GitHub Free. Control: CI budget (PL2-D-41, PL2-D-44); weekly full matrix, changed-ports nightly.

### 3.63 Stamps, payouts, documents and mandates (Finbyx PR-FBX-29; R1-D-52), interfaces and fakes only

- **E and T (money moved without authority, or twice):** Control: every money-moving call needs an idempotency key, an amount in integer paise, an approved maker-checker reference and a user or service initiator; agents are refused by the port check and by the kernel's verb guard; a replayed key returns the first result and another payload under it is refused; payouts never fail over automatically and "accepted" is never final, so a product waits for `settled` (UTR, value date) before booking.
- **I:** account numbers, VPAs, party names and amounts are REGULATED and `IN`; documents and certificates go to the tenant's STORAGE by key and SHA-256 and never sit in the call arguments; the signer IP on a signature record is CONFIDENTIAL evidence only.
- **S (forged callbacks):** every webhook is parsed for an id only and the state is read back.
- **Residual:** no adapter exists, so vendor webhook signatures, the sponsor bank's file formats and the payout rails' transport rules are specified when the owner approves the vendors and provides their sandboxes (OWNER-QUESTIONS #100); until then products refuse these calls above staging (`IPB-PORT-001`).

### 3.62 CAPTCHA and device attestation (ADR-0004; Udofin PR-UDF-028, PR-UDF-029; R1-D-50)

Public forms can ask for a CAPTCHA while the site is under attack; the app's device evidence has a port, with no vendor adapter yet.

- **I (personal data to a CAPTCHA vendor abroad):** Control: the Turnstile and hCaptcha adapters post only `secret` and `response` to `siteverify` (a test checks the form keys); no `remoteip`, login, name or device id; residency GLOBAL, max INTERNAL.
- **S and D (bots on public forms):** Control: `int.captcha.under_attack` turns the gate on at once (PLT_SECURITY_OFFICER, no maker-checker); while on, a missing, refused, reused or unverifiable token is refused (429), and an unreachable vendor refuses too (fail closed). Rate limits stay in place either way.
- **T (a misconfigured secret read as a customer failure):** Control: `invalid-input-secret` and similar codes are errors (IPB-PORT-002) that operations see, never a verdict that blames the customer.
- **S (a fake app or a rooted device registering, PR-UDF-028; adapters R1-D-59):** Control: the `native` adapter, off behind `int.device_attestation.play_integrity.enabled` and `int.device_attestation.app_attest.enabled`. Play Integrity: Google decodes the standard-request token, then the adapter requires all of the following, and refuses a used token (a replay store keyed by its SHA-256):
  - the request hash equal to the server's nonce or DPoP `jkt`;
  - our package and one of our signing-certificate digests;
  - PLAY_RECOGNIZED and LICENSED;
  - at least MEETS_DEVICE_INTEGRITY;
  - a token at most 5 minutes old.

  App Attest is verified locally under C-27: the `x5c` chain to Apple's pinned App Attestation root, the nonce in the credential certificate, the App ID hash, the key id, production only unless allowed, a key registered once, and for every assertion an ES256 signature over this challenge with a growing counter. Every verdict is bound to a server nonce or the DPoP key and never returns a device identifier.

- **I (device data to Google):** Control: only the integrity token is sent (a test checks the body); the service-account assertion is C-24, now widened to the Play Integrity scope only; residency GLOBAL, max INTERNAL. Apple is never called.
- **T (a forged attestation chain):** Control: the root is pinned by its SHA-256 fingerprint, checked at load. A test root can be passed only as `testRoots`, which production wiring never sets.
- **Residual (device attestation):** until the owner's Google Cloud project and Apple account exist, the adapter is off and products fail closed above test environments. The pinned root was taken from two independent public copies with the same fingerprint (`1cb9823b…c932`); the owner confirms it against apple.com before turning App Attest on.
- **Residual:** while the gate is off, bots meet only the rate limits; turning it on blocks a customer whose browser cannot run the widget, so the widget must use the vendor's accessible, non-puzzle mode (IPB-06-40).

### 3.65 V-CIP in our VIDEO_MEET room (M24; R1-D-55)

- **E (a decision by someone other than the official):** Control: `assess` and `complete` accept only the session's `officialRef`; the product authenticates the official (MFA) before calling.
- **I (customer media leaving the tenant):** Control: only frames and the document photo go to the scorer; the recording and all artefacts stay in the tenant's STORAGE by hash; an approval is refused when the room's provider keeps a copy.
- **T (an approval without the checks):** Control: approval needs passed liveness and face match and the customer's geo-tag in India; a decided or dropped session takes no further decision (compare-and-set on its state).
- **Residual:** the geo-tag is what the customer's device reports; the official must also check it on the call. Digio's frame methods and the tenant-owned bucket (#83) are not in place, so V-CIP stays off.

### 3.64 VIDEO_MEET on self-hosted LiveKit with C-26 (M15; R1-D-53)

- **S (forged or widened tokens):** Control: HS256 only in `livekit-token.ts`, only for LiveKit; join tokens are one room, one identity, join grants, ≤ 300 s; server tokens one grant, ≤ 60 s; the secret encodes ≥ 32 random bytes from SECRETS and dev keys are refused; no other HS256 path (a test).
- **S and T (forged webhooks):** Control: HS256 only; issuer, expiry with skew and the body hash checked in constant time; state is read back from the server.
- **I (recordings leaving the tenant):** Control: recordings go only to the room's STORAGE target in one locked single-part write; the egress disk copy is removed after a verified upload; REGULATED rooms keep no provider copy.
- **E (joins from outside policy):** Control: country, anonymised origin, the official's network and the role are checked before a token is issued.
- **Residual:** HS256 is a shared-secret scheme, so anyone holding the deployment secret can mint tokens (kept in SECRETS, one per deployment, rotated with the deployment); the egress worker is unproven until it runs in a deployment.

### 3.66 Chain lanes for the ledger and the capture (`audit/0006`, `0007`; Finbyx PR-FBX-32, ADR-0005, R1-D-58)

Each tenant's ledger and statutory capture are N hash chains (lanes) instead of one, so a tenant's writers no longer queue on one head row. The daily signed anchor seals every lane head.

- **T (an entry edited, removed or inserted):** Control: each lane is the same SHA-256 chain as before (PL1-C-01), and the entry's lane and position are hashed. An edit, a gap, a relinked entry or an entry moved to another lane breaks its lane. Lane heads are written only by the chain owners (`app_migrate`'s trigger, `capture_writer`), and deletes and truncates of them are refused. The verifiers compare each lane's end with its head, so a deleted tail is reported. The format-3 anchor signs every lane head of both chains in KMS and write-once STORAGE.
- **T (history rewritten across lanes after the day's anchor):** Control: the anchor's root over all lane heads no longer matches; formats 1 and 2 still verify.
- **T (a caller steers lanes):** Control: a transaction may pin its lane (`audit.ledger_lane`, `audit.capture_lane`, transaction-local only). That only chooses which lane it waits for: the trigger still locks the lane head and chains the entry.
- **R (ordering across lanes):** Control: no hash links the lanes within a day. Each entry carries its global `seq` and its `occurred_at`, both hashed, and the anchor seals the lanes daily.
- **D (deadlock between two transactions holding a ledger lane and a capture lane in opposite orders):** Control: PostgreSQL aborts one, as with the single heads. Writing the change before its ledger entry keeps the order.
- **D (the upgrade blocks writes):** Control: `audit/0006` locks the two tables for its few statements with a 5 s `lock_timeout`; `audit/0007` builds its indexes concurrently.
- **Residual:** superuser access to PostgreSQL, as for the ledger and the capture (§3.56). Between two anchors, a superuser who rewrites a lane and its head together is caught at the next anchor, not by the head comparison.

### 3.67 BUREAU `cibil` and AA_FIU `setu-aa` against stubs (owner answer #97; R1-D-61)

The first vendor adapters for the ports of §3.10's BUREAU and AA_FIU entry. Data flows: the subject's identifiers to CIBIL and the raw report back into the tenant's STORAGE; the consent template to Setu and FI payloads back into the tenant's STORAGE.

- **I (personal and financial data leaving):** Control: only the subject's identifiers go to CIBIL (never the product's subject reference or consent receipt; a test checks the body), and only the consent template with the customer's AA handle goes to Setu; both over HTTPS to allow-listed hosts in India (`IN`, REGULATED). Raw reports and FI payloads land in REGULATED STORAGE with their SHA-256; results carry no PAN (a PAN-like summary is refused) and only masked accounts (a full number is masked).
- **S (credentials):** Control: CIBIL's member id and API key and Setu's client id, secret and product instance id come from SECRETS; stubs use random values per run.
- **S/T (forged events):** Control: an event is parsed only for our pull id (`bpl_` + ULID) or a consent id the product holds a record of; the state is always read back from the vendor, and an unknown pull or consent is refused.
- **E (pulling or fetching beyond consent):** Control: a pull needs the consent receipt; `cibil` refuses any other CIC; `setu-aa` reads the consent back before every fetch and refuses one that is not ACTIVE, past its expiry, outside its range or for an FI type outside it or revoked here.
- **Residual:** the vendor shapes are stub-shaped until the owner's sandboxes exist, so both adapters are off and `unproven`; webhook signatures and the AA's JWS detached signatures are added with the sandbox documents (the read-back means a forged event only triggers a read). Revoking some FI types is kept by the platform, which stops fetching them, while the consent stays ACTIVE at the AA until it ends or is revoked in full.

### 3.68 Digio mandates and NeSL DDE (owner answer #100; R1-D-62), against stubs and off

- **E and T (a mandate registered or cancelled without authority, or twice):** Control: `assertMoneyCall` before anything else (approved maker-checker reference, user or service initiator, never an agent, idempotency key); the key is claimed in the product's store before Digio is called, a retry reads the first mandate back, another mandate under the key is refused, and after an unknown outcome Digio is searched for the key-derived reference before a second create. `kill.payments` pauses both calls; a production credential is refused outside prod/dr.
- **T (a different ceiling at the bank):** Control: integer paise, sent as an exact decimal; the amount Digio reports is checked back exactly (P-04) on every read with a known request.
- **I (debtor account data):** Control: the account number, IFSC and contact come from the product's resolver per call, go only to Digio (`IN`, REGULATED) and are neither returned nor stored; the approval reference is not sent. DDE documents leave only after their SHA-256 matches, and the executed PDF goes to the tenant's STORAGE with its hash.
- **S (forged callbacks):** Control: our C-19 URL secret; the body is used only for the id, and the mandate or DDE record is read back from the vendor.
- **Residual:** vendor shapes are stub-shaped until the owner's Digio mandate and NeSL accounts exist, so the adapters stay off and `unproven`; Digio documents no webhook signature here, so the URL secret must stay out of ingress logs. Two concurrent first calls with one DDE key can both register before either is found (the product serialises a key, as for every idempotent call).

### 3.69 The mandate approval consumed by the approvals engine (owner answer #100's gap; R1-D-66)

- **E (an approval reference forged, reused, or taken from another tenant, mandate or amount):** Control: `digio-mandate` calls the approvals engine's `consumeMandateApproval` before Digio is called to register or cancel. It refuses unless the request is on `payments.mandate.change` at R3 or above, approved and past its delay, not yet consumed, visible in the caller's tenant under RLS (and its `tenant_id` equal), and its payload is exactly the call's subject: the operation, the mandate (the registration's idempotency key, or the mandate id to cancel) and the ceiling in integer paise (with its canonical digest). It then marks the request executed and ledgers `approvals.request.consume` in the same transaction under the row lock, so two concurrent calls cannot both consume it (integration test). An agent caller is refused. The product's own maker-checker still runs first.
- **T (the action executed without a vendor call, or by an executor):** Control: the engine registers `payments.mandate.change` itself, never executed on approval; `execute()` refuses it, and registering it with an executor or auto-execution throws.
- **Residual:** once consumed, an approval cannot cover a second vendor call, so a registration or cancellation that must be made again after an unknown outcome or a vendor failure needs a fresh approval (fail-safe). Amendment has no port method yet; when it comes it consumes an `amend` approval the same way.

### 3.70 CI only on the `ci:run` label (R1-D-64)

- **E (a required check satisfied without running):** pull-request CI starts only when a label is added. A job skipped by a condition reports success to the required checks, so any label other than `ci:run` cancels the run (its jobs report cancelled, which never satisfies a required check) instead of skipping it; the required checks and the ruleset are unchanged, and pushes to `main` and the release run as before. A cancelled run does not replace an earlier green or red result on the same commit with a pass.
- **Residual:** a push after the label leaves the PR's checks pending until `ci:run` is removed and added again, which blocks the merge (fail-safe).

### 3.71 Tenant creation for a provisional id (`TenantBootstrap`; PR-LDC-12, R1-D-67)

- **E (a tenant created outside provisioning in production):** Control: `bootstrap` is refused in `prod`, `dr` and `pre-prod` (`SEED_REFUSED_ENVS`); there only `provision` runs, called by the Operations Hub adapter after it verifies the signed `TenantProvisioned` (§3.72), and its ledger entry names the provisioning request. Both need a platform context with a PLT_* role, which the kernel validates; the tenant row's insert policy already requires platform scope.
- **T (a provisional id reused for another tenant, or a partial tenant):** Control: idempotent per id (a different request under the id is refused); the tenant row, its settings, the product's own rows and the ledger entry commit or roll back together (integration test). Initial settings go through `SettingsService.setInitial`, which keeps `set`'s validation, tighten check and ledger entry, is refused outside a tenant's creation, and refuses settings under maker-checker.
- **Residual:** `provision` trusts its caller to have verified the event; it is a library call, so a product that calls it from anywhere else bypasses the Hub's maker-checker. The guide names the adapter as its only caller.

### 3.72 The Operations Hub provisioning adapter (`TenantProvisioned`; PR-LDC-11, R1-D-68)

- **S (a forged or replayed provisioning event creates a tenant):** Control: the detached JWS is verified against the Hub's pinned key ring before anything is stored (ES256 only, `kid`, `iat` window, `jti` claimed once; C-22), and the source must be the Hub's; the JWS is kept beside the event for later re-verification.
- **E (a tenant for another product or environment, or a PRODUCTION tenant without its contracts):** Control: the payload must name this product and this deployment's environment; PRODUCTION needs the MSA, DPA and OUT clause-matrix refs (`regulatory-guard`, Spec §6.29); the envelope's environment must match the inbox's.
- **T (one request provisioned twice):** Control: the inbox stores the event by `(source, id)` and the request id as its business key, so a second event for the request is never processed; the tenant id is the provisional id or one derived from the request id, and `provision` is idempotent per id.
- **I (the first admin's work email):** Control: accepted only with `legalbasis = CONTRACT_PERFORMANCE_VENDOR`; it is passed to the product's hook and not stored by the platform.
- **Residual:** the payload's field names follow the Spec's wording until the Hub publishes its registry schema (`[VERIFY]`). The request half and `ProvisioningFailed` are §3.74 (Spec v1.3).

### 3.73 Per-tenant templates and delivery reports by vendor reference (PR-FBX-33, PR-LDC-15; R1-D-69)

- **I and T (a message sent under another tenant's sender, DLT entity or template):** Control: the resolver runs at delivery in the delivering tenant's context, `dispatchDue` included (each due delivery is delivered in its own tenant's service context); `null` fails the delivery closed and a template of another channel is refused, so a missing tenant template never falls back silently to someone else's.
- **T and I (a delivery report applied to the wrong tenant, or a cross-tenant read):** Control: the dispatcher's service reader (`svc_notify_dispatch`) reads only `tenant_id`, `channel`, `adapter` and `vendor_ref` among the new columns (column grants; never an address or content), each read is ledgered in every tenant it touched, and the update then runs in that tenant's context under RLS. A reference found in two tenants is applied to neither; an unknown one is dropped and counted. Webhook signatures are verified by the adapter before routing, as before.
- **Residual:** a vendor that reuses a message id across accounts would make its reports `ambiguous` (dropped); the counts make it visible.

### 3.74 Provisioning requested by a product and refused by the Hub (Spec v1.3; PR-040, #105; R1-D-70)

- **S (a forged `ProvisioningFailed` aborts a real sign-up, or a forged `ProvisioningRequested` reaches the Hub):** Control: `ProvisioningFailed` is verified exactly as `TenantProvisioned` is (§3.72: the Hub's pinned key ring, ES256, `kid`, `iat` window, `jti` once); `ProvisioningRequested` is signed with the product's ecosystem key from KMS at delivery, never at write time, so no long-lived JWS sits in the outbox.
- **T (one request ends twice, e.g. a tenant created after a failure was acted on):** Control: `TenantProvisioned` and `ProvisioningFailed` share the request id as business key with `exclusiveWith`, under an advisory transaction lock, so only the first stored of either is processed; the other is acknowledged as `business_duplicate` and ledgered. A request id reused with another payload is refused (`IPB-PORT-004`).
- **I (personal data in the failure, or a provisional tenant's contact data kept after a failure):** Control: `detail` is refused when it carries an email, a mobile number, a PAN or a long number; `ProvisioningRequested` carries business contact data only, with `legalbasis` claimed only when the first-admin email is sent. The product purges the provisional tenant and its first-admin contact data within 30 days of `failed_at` (`purgeBy`), ledgered, unless a retry is open (Spec v1.3 retention rule; DPDP Act 2023 s.8(7)).
- **E (a product acknowledges failures it does not act on):** Control: without an `onFailed` hook the adapter refuses `ProvisioningFailed`, so the Hub keeps resending it rather than it being dropped.
- **Residual:** the personal-data check is a pattern list (a false positive only refuses the event, which the Hub resends without the detail); the purge itself is the product's code, checked by its own tests.

### 3.75 CEL string functions and linear-time patterns (PR-LDC-13, R1-D-71)

- **D (ReDoS: a rule author's pattern makes evaluation super-linear):** Control: `matches` takes only a string literal from a static subset checked when the draft is saved, before publication (no back-references, look-around, atomic or possessive constructs, no quantified group holding a quantifier or an alternation; at most 256 characters). The production engines (`zen`, `json-rules`) search with `re2js` 2.8.8, a pure JavaScript RE2 port, linear by construction; the text is at most 4096 characters, and the evaluation time limit still applies. `evaluateCel`, which runs the CEL library's backtracking RegExp, refuses a non-literal or out-of-subset pattern and any input string over 4096 characters.
- **T (engines disagreeing on a string row):** Control: the subset leaves out every construct whose meaning differs between RE2 and a JavaScript RegExp (`.`, negated classes, `\D`, `\W`, `\s`, `\S`, `\B`, `\x`, `\u`, `\p`); ZEN never sees a pattern (its result is passed in as a boolean input); strings must be well-formed UTF-16. A randomised test over 4,000 patterns checks re2js against RegExp, and a 600-input differential test checks the three engines against each other.
- **Residual:** adjacent ambiguous quantifiers (e.g. `\d+\d+`) are polynomial on a backtracking engine; they run there only in the memory walk (tests and validation) and in `evaluateCel`, both bounded by 4096-character strings. `re2js` (MIT, no dependencies) was already in the lockfile through `unleash-client`.

### 3.76 Platform purpose notices, consent read by principal and the #95 purge (PR-LDC-14, #95; R1-D-72)

- **T and E (a tenant creates, edits or retires a notice every tenant relies on):** Control: platform notices are `tnt_platform` rows whose insert and update policies require a platform-scope transaction, which the kernel grants only to PLT_* principals; the service also requires a PLT_DPO to draft and a different PLT_LEGAL_REVIEWER to approve and retire. Approved wording is immutable and its SHA-256 is computed by the database (`consent/0003`); tenants never see drafts.
- **I (one tenant's consents read from another):** Control: `latestByPrincipal` runs in the call's tenant under RLS and filters on it. The sweep's finder (`svc_consent_sweep`) reads only status rows' `tenant_id`, `updated_at` and `relationship_ended_at` (column grants), never a purpose record, and each read is ledgered in every tenant it touched.
- **T and repudiation (data deleted early or outside the approved rule):** Control: the later of #95's two clocks is enforced in PostgreSQL's restrictive delete policy: a record goes only once the reported relationship end and any later withdrawal are both 8 years past, never while the relationship continues and never under a legal hold; the 8 years are fixed in SQL; the relationship end is an explicit, ledgered product report that defaults to "not ended". Deletes also need the purge action and the sweep's principal (trigger); every other delete or update is refused. Each batch is ledgered with counts only. A notice version still cited in any tenant is protected by the foreign key.
- **Residual:** a product that reports a wrong, back-dated relationship end shortens retention for that person (ledgered as `consent.subject.relationship_end`). The purge is a ledgered sweep without IPB-02-24's 30-day tombstone window, as the #48 and #57 purges are (the owner's approval of the rule stands for the purge's approval, PL5-D-05).

### 3.77 Field encryption over the KMS port (`fieldCryptoForTenant`; PR-LDC-16, R1-D-73)

- **I (one tenant's sealed field read under another tenant):** Control: the key id is derived only from the validated tenant id and the adapter's fixed convention (C-15); no caller supplies a key id. A `TenantFieldCrypto` refuses any call naming another tenant before the KMS is called; a ciphertext relabelled with another key id still fails at the KMS (another key, another `tenant_id` context, C-14) and at the field AAD (tenant ‖ table ‖ column ‖ row). In AWS the key policy allows `kms:GenerateDataKey` and `kms:Decrypt` to the runtime role only with that tenant and `purpose: field` in the encryption context.
- **T and S (an alias repointed at another tenant's key, or an unknown adapter):** Control: alias and key administration is not granted to the runtime role; a repointed alias would wrap under a key whose policy refuses the other tenant's context, so the seal fails rather than crossing tenants; an adapter without a convention is refused (`IPB-PORT-001`), so nothing falls back to a shared key.
- **D (the tenant key missing, disabled or pending deletion):** Control: sealing and opening fail closed (`IPB-KMS-001`), never to plaintext; keys are created idempotently when the tenant is created.
